  "old_string": "Hybrid recurrent architectures that interleave attention layers with linear recurrence are increasingly standard, yet how attention affects per-example behavior across different recurrent backbones remains poorly quantified. We conduct a systematic analysis using the controlled model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}: three architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2) at five attention ratios and two scales (340M and 1.3B parameters), evaluated on 10 benchmarks with 5{,}153 per-example comparisons on LAMBADA. Despite aggregate convergence, per-example helped and hurt sets show low overlap across architectures (Jaccard similarity $\\leq 0.11$, approximately twice the independence baseline), and this overlap saturates at minimal (4\\%) attention. These results quantify predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} in hybrid architecture design: aggregate equivalence does not imply per-example behavioral interchangeability.",
  "new_string": "Hybrid recurrent architectures that interleave attention layers with linear recurrence are increasingly standard, yet how attention affects per-example behavior across different recurrent backbones remains poorly quantified. We conduct a systematic analysis using the controlled model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}: three architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2) at five attention ratios and two scales (340M and 1.3B parameters), evaluated on 10 benchmarks with per-example analysis on LAMBADA ($N = 5{,}153$) and ARC-Easy ($N = 2{,}376$). Aggregate convergence is task-specific: only LAMBADA shows monotonic reduction in cross-architecture variance ($\\rho = -1.0$, BCa 95\\% CI $[-1.0, -0.7]$; $\\sigma$ drops 79\\%; Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$), complete at 1.3B but non-monotonic at 340M. Per-example predictive multiplicity, however, is general: on both benchmarks, architectures help and hurt different examples when attention is added (Jaccard $\\leq 0.13$, all $p < .01$; ambiguity $\\alpha \\approx 0.21$--$0.30$ stable across attention ratios). On LAMBADA, aggregate and per-example trajectories are decoupled: $\\sigma$ decreases gradually while per-example overlap saturates at 4\\% attention. On ARC-Easy, per-example divergence persists without any aggregate convergence ($\\rho = -0.20$, n.s.). These results establish predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} as a general property of hybrid architectures, independent of aggregate performance equalization."
}

    "originalFile": "\\documentclass[11pt]{article}\n\n\\usepackage[review]{acl}\n\n\\usepackage{times}\n\\usepackage{latexsym}\n\\usepackage[T1]{fontenc}\n\\usepackage[utf8]{inputenc}\n\\usepackage{microtype}\n\\usepackage{graphicx}\n\\usepackage{booktabs}\n\\usepackage{amsmath}\n\\usepackage{multirow}\n\\usepackage{xcolor}\n\n\\title{Task-Specific Convergence and Per-Example Predictive Multiplicity in Hybrid Recurrent Architectures}\n\n\\author{Anonymous}\n\n\\begin{document}\n\\maketitle\n\n\\begin{abstract}\nHybrid recurrent architectures that interleave attention layers with linear recurrence are increasingly standard, yet how attention affects per-example behavior across different recurrent backbones remains poorly quantified. We conduct a systematic analysis using the controlled model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}: three architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2) at five attention ratios and two scales (340M and 1.3B parameters), evaluated on 10 benchmarks with 5{,}153 per-example comparisons on LAMBADA. Within each architecture, pure-model confidence on the target token predicts which examples are helped or hurt ($d = 0.73$--$0.92$), yet this predictability does not transfer across architectures. Aggregate performance equalizes progressively, while per-example specificity is established from the earliest attention allocation. These results quantify predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} in hybrid architecture design: aggregate equivalence does not imply per-example behavioral interchangeability.\n\\end{abstract}\n\n\\input{sections/introduction}\n\n\\input{sections/related_work}\n\n\\input{sections/method}\n\n\\input{sections/experiments}\n\n\\input{sections/conclusion}\n\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\nOur analysis has several limitations that constrain the scope of its conclusions.\n\n\\paragraph{Statistical power.} With only $n = 5$ attention ratios, the minimum achievable two-tailed $p$-value under an exact permutation test is approximately 0.008. This ceiling limits our ability to detect weak convergence effects and contributes to the Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$ for LAMBADA not reaching conventional significance. We report confidence intervals as a complementary characterization to partially address this constraint.\n\n\\paragraph{Per-example scope.} Our per-example decomposition is limited to LAMBADA, the only benchmark with statistically supported convergence. Extending helped/hurt analysis to benchmarks without convergence would not test the central claim, but examining per-example effects on tasks with moderate but non-significant convergence (e.g., ARC-Easy, MMLU) could reveal whether the decoupling pattern generalizes.\n\n\\paragraph{Scale coverage.} We compare two scale points (340M and 1.3B parameters). The contrast between complete convergence at 1.3B and partial convergence at 340M is a binary observation, not a scaling law. Intermediate and larger scales would be needed to characterize the functional relationship between model capacity and convergence.\n\n\\paragraph{Architecture coverage.} Our study examines three recurrent architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2) from one model zoo. Other hybrid families, including Jamba, RWKV-6, and Hymba, use different mixing strategies that may produce different per-example patterns. Generalization beyond these three architectures requires additional controlled experiments.\n\n\\paragraph{Observational design.} Our attention weight analysis is descriptive, not causal. We report cosine similarities and entropy differences between helped and hurt examples but cannot determine whether attention patterns cause the per-example effects or merely correlate with them. Interventional methods such as attention head ablation would be necessary to establish causal links.\n\n\\paragraph{Pre-training only.} All models in our study are evaluated after pre-training without fine-tuning. Per-example effects may change under task-specific adaptation, and the decoupling pattern we observe may not persist after fine-tuning.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work analyzes publicly available pretrained language models from the model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}. No new data collection, human subjects research, or model training is involved. We identify per-example behavioral differences across architectures, which may inform practitioners about the limitations of aggregate benchmark comparisons but does not introduce new risks.\n\n\\bibliography{references}\n\n\\appendix\n\\input{sections/appendix}\n\n\\end{document}\n",

Per-example effects may change under task-specific adaptation, and the decoupling pattern we observe may not persist after fine-tuning.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work analyzes publicly available pretrained language models from the model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}. No new data collection, human subjects research, or model training is involved. We identify per-example behavioral differences across architectures, which may inform practitioners about the limitations of aggregate benchmark comparisons but does not introduce new risks.\n\n\\bibliography{references}\n\n\\appendix\n\\input{sections/appendix}\n\n\\end{document}\n",

          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Statistical power.} With only $n = 5$ attention ratios, the minimum achievable two-tailed $p$-value under an exact permutation test is approximately 0.008. This ceiling limits our ability to detect weak convergence effects and contributes to the Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$ for LAMBADA not reaching conventional significance. We report confidence intervals as a complementary characterization to partially address this constraint.",

### Tool result

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01HBbPfhyTfZqnASoiq4Q9AW`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	When attention layers are added to three different recurrent architectures, their LAMBADA performance becomes near-identical: cross-architecture standard deviation drops from 0.024 to 0.005, a 79\% reduction. Yet this aggregate convergence hides an asymmetry at the per-example level. The three architectures reach similar overall accuracy by helping and hurting different examples with low overlap (pairwise Jaccard at most 0.11, approximately twice the independence baseline). Two models can agree on a benchmark score while disagreeing on which inputs they get right. This tension between aggregate agreement and per-example disagreement arises in the context of a rapid architectural shift toward hybrid recurrent-attention models, which interleave attention layers within linear recurrent backbones. Jamba \citep{lieber2024jamba}, Griffin \citep{de2024griffin}, RWKV-6 \citep{peng2024eagle}, and RecurrentGemma \citep{botev2024recurrentgemma} each combine recurrence with attention using different mixing strategies. These systems now serve as production-grade alternatives to pure Transformers, and the practical question of how much attention to include is typically answered by comparing aggregate benchmark numbers. \citet{wang2025hybrid} provide a controlled model zoo spanning three recurrent architectures, five attention ratios, and two parameter scales, which enables systematic study of how attention allocation affects these hybrid designs.
4	
5	Most evaluations of hybrid architectures compare aggregate benchmark scores and report that a given attention ratio achieves a certain accuracy averaged across tasks or averaged across architectures. This practice can mask substantial per-example behavioral diversity. \citet{marx2020predictive} formalized this risk as predictive multiplicity: a prediction problem admits competing models with conflicting predictions on individual inputs. \citet{damour2022underspecification} documented a related phenomenon, underspecification, showing that models trained from the same pipeline with different random seeds can disagree on individual predictions despite equivalent aggregate performance. \citet{mccoy2020berts} demonstrated a concrete NLP instance: BERT models trained from different random seeds diverge on specific linguistic constructions. These studies examine variation from initialization or training noise within a single architecture. Whether predictive multiplicity extends to hybrid recurrent architectures, where the source of variation is the recurrent backbone itself, remains unquantified. The question matters because practitioners increasingly choose between recurrent backbones based on aggregate benchmarks; if architectures that score identically in aggregate behave differently on individual inputs, such choices carry hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction.
6	
7	We present the first systematic per-example analysis of how attention affects cross-architecture agreement in hybrid recurrent models. Using the model zoo of \citet{wang2025hybrid}, we compare three architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2) at five attention ratios (0\% to 25\%) and two scales (340M and 1.3B parameters). These three architectures represent distinct approaches to linear recurrence: gated delta rules, multi-scale exponential decay, and gated state expansion. We evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks spanning retrieval, comprehension, reasoning, and mixed task categories. On LAMBADA, we conduct a full per-example decomposition across 5{,}153 examples, where each example is classified as helped, hurt, or unchanged when attention is added to each architecture. We then measure cross-architecture overlap in these per-example effect sets using Jaccard similarity. Figure~\ref{fig:decoupling} previews our central result: aggregate convergence and per-example divergence follow different trajectories as attention ratio increases.
8	
9	\begin{figure}[t]
10	\centering
11	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_decoupling_hero.pdf}
12	\caption{Aggregate convergence and per-example divergence are decoupled. (a) Cross-architecture standard deviation on LAMBADA decreases gradually with attention ratio at 1.3B (solid) but partially rebounds at 340M (dashed). (b) Pairwise Jaccard similarity of per-example helped/hurt sets saturates at the first attention level (4\%) and remains flat.}
13	\label{fig:decoupling}
14	\end{figure}
15	
16	Our analysis yields three findings. First, attention-driven convergence is task-specific. Of 10 benchmarks, only LAMBADA exhibits monotonic reduction in cross-architecture variance ($\rho = -1.0$, BCa 95\% CI $[-1.0, -0.7]$; cross-architecture $\sigma$ drops 79\%). LAMBADA \citep{paperno2016lambada} is a pure context-retrieval task that requires predicting the final word of a passage from long-range dependencies; attention provides direct token-level access to the full context, which offers a natural explanation for why convergence occurs on this task and not others. Benchmarks that involve reasoning or comprehension show no convergence, with all $|\rho| \leq 0.7$ and $p \geq .233$. The Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$ does not survive strict correction at $\alpha = 0.05$; the confidence interval, which excludes zero, provides a complementary characterization of the effect. Second, convergence depends on scale. At 1.3B parameters the $\sigma$ trajectory is monotonically decreasing, but at 340M it is non-monotonic: $\sigma$ partially rebounds at the highest attention ratio, with only 36\% net reduction versus 79\% at 1.3B. This contrast provides a preliminary observation that sufficient model capacity may facilitate complete equalization on retrieval. Third, and central to our title-level claim, aggregate convergence and per-example divergence are decoupled. Cross-architecture $\sigma$ on LAMBADA decreases gradually across attention ratios ($0.024 \to 0.016 \to 0.014 \to 0.006 \to 0.005$), but pairwise Jaccard similarity of per-example helped and hurt sets saturates at the first attention level (4\%) and remains flat at approximately 0.095. Within each architecture, pure-model confidence on the target token predicts which examples are helped or hurt ($d = 0.73$--$0.92$), but this predictability does not transfer across architectures. These two quantities operate on different timescales: aggregate performance equalizes progressively, while per-example specificity is established from the earliest attention allocation and does not change with more attention.
17	
18	Our contributions are as follows:
19	\begin{itemize}
20	    \item We quantify task-specific convergence: only retrieval tasks (LAMBADA) show attention-driven cross-architecture convergence at 1.3B scale; reasoning and comprehension benchmarks do not (\S\ref{ssec:task_convergence}, Table~\ref{tab:convergence}, Figure~\ref{fig:task_specificity}).
21	    \item We observe scale-dependent convergence: the monotonic convergence pattern at 1.3B becomes non-monotonic at 340M, a preliminary observation that sufficient scale may facilitate complete equalization (\S\ref{ssec:scale_convergence}).
22	    \item We demonstrate that aggregate convergence and per-example divergence are decoupled: attention reduces cross-architecture variance gradually, but per-example overlap saturates at the first attention level and does not increase further, constituting a form of predictive multiplicity arising from architectural variation (\S\ref{ssec:per_example}, \S\ref{ssec:decoupling}, Figure~\ref{fig:decoupling}).
23	\end{itemize}
24	
```

These systems now serve as production-grade alternatives to pure Transformers, and the practical question of how much attention to include is typically answered by comparing aggregate benchmark numbers. \\citet{wang2025hybrid} provide a controlled model zoo spanning three recurrent architectures, five attention ratios, and two parameter scales, which enables systematic study of how attention allocation affects these hybrid designs.\n\nMost evaluations of hybrid architectures compare aggregate benchmark scores and report that a given attention ratio achieves a certain accuracy averaged across tasks or averaged across architectures. This practice can mask substantial per-example behavioral diversity. \\citet{marx2020predictive} formalized this risk as predictive multiplicity: a prediction problem admits competing models with conflicting predictions on individual inputs. \\citet{damour2022underspecification} documented a related phenomenon, underspecification, showing that models trained from the same pipeline with different random seeds can disagree on individual predictions despite equivalent aggregate performance. \\citet{mccoy2020berts} demonstrated a concrete NLP instance: BERT models trained from different random seeds diverge on specific linguistic constructions. These studies examine variation from initialization or training noise within a single architecture. Whether predictive multiplicity extends to hybrid recurrent architectures, where the source of variation is the recurrent backbone itself, remains unquantified. The question matters because practitioners increasingly choose between recurrent backbones based on aggregate benchmarks; if architectures that score identically in aggregate behave differently on individual inputs, such choices carry hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction.\n\nWe present the first systematic per-example analysis of how attention affects cross-architecture agreement in hybrid recurrent models. Using the model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}, we compare three architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2) at five attention ratios (0\\% to 25\\%) and two scales (340M and 1.3B parameters). These three architectures represent distinct approaches to linear recurrence: gated delta rules, multi-scale exponential decay, and gated state expansion. We evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks spanning retrieval, comprehension, reasoning, and mixed task categories. On LAMBADA, we conduct a full per-example decomposition across 5{,}153 examples, where each example is classified as helped, hurt, or unchanged when attention is added to each architecture. We then measure cross-architecture overlap in these per-example effect sets using Jaccard similarity. Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling} previews our central result: aggregate convergence and per-example divergence follow different trajectories as attention ratio increases.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_decoupling_hero.pdf}\n\\caption{Aggregate convergence and per-example divergence are decoupled. (a) Cross-architecture standard deviation on LAMBADA decreases gradually with attention ratio at 1.3B (solid) but partially rebounds at 340M (dashed). (b) Pairwise Jaccard similarity of per-example helped/hurt sets saturates at the first attention level (4\\%) and remains flat.}\n\\label{fig:decoupling}\n\\end{figure}\n\nOur analysis yields three findings. First, attention-driven convergence is task-specific. Of 10 benchmarks, only LAMBADA exhibits monotonic reduction in cross-architecture variance ($\\rho = -1.0$, BCa 95\\% CI $[-1.0, -0.7]$; cross-architecture $\\sigma$ drops 79\\%). LAMBADA \\citep{paperno2016lambada} is a pure context-retrieval task that requires predicting the final word of a passage from long-range dependencies; attention provides direct token-level access to the full context, which offers a natural explanation for why convergence occurs on this task and not others. Benchmarks that involve reasoning or comprehension show no convergence, with all $|\\rho| \\leq 0.7$ and $p \\geq .233$. The Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$ does not survive strict correction at $\\alpha = 0.05$; the confidence interval, which excludes zero, provides a complementary characterization of the effect. Second, convergence depends on scale. At 1.3B parameters the $\\sigma$ trajectory is monotonically decreasing, but at 340M it is non-monotonic: $\\sigma$ partially rebounds at the highest attention ratio, with only 36\\% net reduction versus 79\\% at 1.3B. This contrast provides a preliminary observation that sufficient model capacity may facilitate complete equalization on retrieval. Third, and central to our title-level claim, aggregate convergence and per-example divergence are decoupled. Cross-architecture $\\sigma$ on LAMBADA decreases gradually across attention ratios ($0.024 \\to 0.016 \\to 0.014 \\to 0.006 \\to 0.005$), but pairwise Jaccard similarity of per-example helped and hurt sets saturates at the first attention level (4\\%) and remains flat at approximately 0.095. Within each architecture, pure-model confidence on the target token predicts which examples are helped or hurt ($d = 0.73$--$0.92$), but this predictability does not transfer across architectures. These two quantities operate on different timescales: aggregate performance equalizes progressively, while per-example specificity is established from the earliest attention allocation and does not change with more attention.\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item We quantify task-specific convergence: only retrieval tasks (LAMBADA) show attention-driven cross-architecture convergence at 1.3B scale; reasoning and comprehension benchmarks do not (\\S\\ref{ssec:task_convergence}, Table~\\ref{tab:convergence}, Figure~\\ref{fig:task_specificity}).\n    \\item We observe scale-dependent convergence: the monotonic convergence pattern at 1.3B becomes non-monotonic at 340M, a preliminary observation that sufficient scale may facilitate complete equalization (\\S\\ref{ssec:scale_convergence}).\n    \\item We demonstrate that aggregate convergence and per-example divergence are decoupled: attention reduces cross-architecture variance gradually, but per-example overlap saturates at the first attention level and does not increase further, constituting a form of predictive multiplicity arising from architectural variation (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}, \\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}, Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling}).\n\\end{itemize}\n",

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "3f5d3cf5-c2ed-43a6-97e2-d8af1974ed0f",

  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_2/projects/entrainment_gated_ft/docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex",
    "oldString": "    \\item We demonstrate that aggregate convergence and per-example divergence are decoupled: attention reduces cross-architecture variance gradually, but per-example overlap saturates at the first attention level and does not increase further, constituting a form of predictive multiplicity arising from architectural variation (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}, \\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}, Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling}).",
    "newString": "    \\item We demonstrate that per-example predictive multiplicity is a general property of hybrid architectures (ambiguity $\\alpha \\approx 0.21$--$0.30$ across retrieval and reasoning tasks), independent of whether aggregate performance converges (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}, \\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}, Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nWhen attention layers are added to three different recurrent architectures, their LAMBADA performance becomes near-identical: cross-architecture standard deviation drops from 0.024 to 0.005, a 79\\% reduction. Yet this aggregate convergence hides an asymmetry at the per-example level. The three architectures reach similar overall accuracy by helping and hurting different examples with low overlap (pairwise Jaccard at most 0.11, approximately twice the independence baseline). Two models can agree on a benchmark score while disagreeing on which inputs they get right. This tension between aggregate agreement and per-example disagreement arises in the context of a rapid architectural shift toward hybrid recurrent-attention models, which interleave attention layers within linear recurrent backbones. Jamba \\citep{lieber2024jamba}, Griffin \\citep{de2024griffin}, RWKV-6 \\citep{peng2024eagle}, and RecurrentGemma \\citep{botev2024recurrentgemma} each combine recurrence with attention using different mixing strategies. These systems now serve as production-grade alternatives to pure Transformers, and the practical question of how much attention to include is typically answered by comparing aggregate benchmark numbers. \\citet{wang2025hybrid} provide a controlled model zoo spanning three recurrent architectures, five attention ratios, and two parameter scales, which enables systematic study of how attention allocation affects these hybrid designs.\n\nMost evaluations of hybrid architectures compare aggregate benchmark scores and report that a given attention ratio achieves a certain accuracy averaged across tasks or averaged across architectures. This practice can mask substantial per-example behavioral diversity. \\citet{marx2020predictive} formalized this risk as predictive multiplicity: a prediction problem admits competing models with conflicting predictions on individual inputs. \\citet{damour2022underspecification} documented a related phenomenon, underspecification, showing that models trained from the same pipeline with different random seeds can disagree on individual predictions despite equivalent aggregate performance. \\citet{mccoy2020berts} demonstrated a concrete NLP instance: BERT models trained from different random seeds diverge on specific linguistic constructions. These studies examine variation from initialization or training noise within a single architecture. Whether predictive multiplicity extends to hybrid recurrent architectures, where the source of variation is the recurrent backbone itself, remains unquantified. The question matters because practitioners increasingly choose between recurrent backbones based on aggregate benchmarks; if architectures that score identically in aggregate behave differently on individual inputs, such choices carry hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction.\n\nWe present the first systematic per-example analysis of how attention affects cross-architecture agreement in hybrid recurrent models. Using the model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}, we compare three architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2) at five attention ratios (0\\% to 25\\%) and two scales (340M and 1.3B parameters). These three architectures represent distinct approaches to linear recurrence: gated delta rules, multi-scale exponential decay, and gated state expansion. We evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks spanning retrieval, comprehension, reasoning, and mixed task categories. On LAMBADA ($N = 5{,}153$) and ARC-Easy ($N = 2{,}376$), we conduct per-example decompositions, classifying each example as helped, hurt, or unchanged when attention is added, and measure cross-architecture overlap using Jaccard similarity. Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling} previews our central result: aggregate convergence and per-example divergence follow different trajectories as attention ratio increases.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_decoupling_hero.pdf}\n\\caption{Aggregate convergence and per-example divergence are decoupled. (a) Cross-architecture standard deviation on LAMBADA decreases gradually with attention ratio at 1.3B (solid) but partially rebounds at 340M (dashed). (b) Pairwise Jaccard similarity of per-example helped/hurt sets saturates at the first attention level (4\\%) and remains flat.}\n\\label{fig:decoupling}\n\\end{figure}\n\nOur analysis yields three findings. First, attention-driven convergence is task-specific. Of 10 benchmarks, only LAMBADA exhibits monotonic reduction in cross-architecture variance ($\\rho = -1.0$, BCa 95\\% CI $[-1.0, -0.7]$; cross-architecture $\\sigma$ drops 79\\%). LAMBADA \\citep{paperno2016lambada} is a pure context-retrieval task that requires predicting the final word of a passage from long-range dependencies; attention provides direct token-level access to the full context, which offers a natural explanation for why convergence occurs on this task and not others. Benchmarks that involve reasoning or comprehension show no convergence, with all $|\\rho| \\leq 0.7$ and $p \\geq .233$. The Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$ does not survive strict correction at $\\alpha = 0.05$; the confidence interval, which excludes zero, provides a complementary characterization of the effect. Second, convergence depends on scale. At 1.3B parameters the $\\sigma$ trajectory is monotonically decreasing, but at 340M it is non-monotonic: $\\sigma$ partially rebounds at the highest attention ratio, with only 36\\% net reduction versus 79\\% at 1.3B. This contrast provides a preliminary observation that sufficient model capacity may facilitate complete equalization on retrieval. Third, and central to our title-level claim, per-example predictive multiplicity is a general property of hybrid architectures, independent of aggregate convergence. On LAMBADA, aggregate $\\sigma$ decreases gradually ($0.024 \\to 0.005$) while pairwise Jaccard of per-example effects saturates at 4\\% attention (${\\sim}0.095$)---the two quantities are decoupled. On ARC-Easy, no aggregate convergence occurs ($\\rho = -0.20$, n.s.), yet per-example overlap is comparably low (Jaccard 0.07--0.13, all $p < .01$) and the ambiguity $\\alpha$ of \\citet{marx2020predictive} is 0.21. Across both benchmarks, $\\alpha$ is stable at 0.21--0.30 regardless of attention ratio.\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item We quantify task-specific convergence: only retrieval tasks (LAMBADA) show attention-driven cross-architecture convergence at 1.3B scale; reasoning and comprehension benchmarks do not (\\S\\ref{ssec:task_convergence}, Table~\\ref{tab:convergence}, Figure~\\ref{fig:task_specificity}).\n    \\item We observe scale-dependent convergence: the monotonic convergence pattern at 1.3B becomes non-monotonic at 340M, a preliminary observation that sufficient scale may facilitate complete equalization (\\S\\ref{ssec:scale_convergence}).\n    \\item We demonstrate that aggregate convergence and per-example divergence are decoupled: attention reduces cross-architecture variance gradually, but per-example overlap saturates at the first attention level and does not increase further, constituting a form of predictive multiplicity arising from architectural variation (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}, \\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}, Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling}).\n\\end{itemize}\n",

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_2/projects/entrainment_gated_ft/docs/paper/sections/experiments.tex",
  "old_string": "This structured per-example disagreement despite similar aggregate performance constitutes predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} arising from architectural variation rather than random initialization \\citep{damour2022underspecification}.\n\n\n\\subsection{Decoupling}",
  "new_string": "This structured per-example disagreement despite similar aggregate performance constitutes predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} arising from architectural variation rather than random initialization \\citep{damour2022underspecification}.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-task generalization.} To test whether per-example divergence depends on aggregate convergence, we repeat the analysis on ARC-Easy ($N = 2{,}376$), a mixed benchmark with no aggregate convergence ($\\rho = -0.20$, $p = .783$; $\\sigma$ flat at ${\\sim}0.10$). Pairwise Jaccard ranges from 0.067 to 0.134 (all 24 pair $\\times$ ratio combinations $p < .01$, permutation test). Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity} reports the ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive}---the fraction of examples with cross-architecture disagreement. Ambiguity is substantial on both benchmarks ($\\alpha \\approx 0.21$--$0.30$) and stable across attention ratios, confirming that per-example multiplicity is not contingent on aggregate convergence.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Predictive multiplicity at 1.3B scale (h3-1). $\\alpha$: ambiguity \\citep{marx2020predictive}. $\\bar{J}$: mean pairwise Jaccard. $\\rho_\\sigma$: Spearman correlation of $\\sigma$ with attention ratio.}\n\\label{tab:multiplicity}\n\\begin{tabular}{lrcccc}\n\\toprule\n & $N$ & $\\alpha$ & $\\bar{J}_{\\text{helped}}$ & $\\bar{J}_{\\text{hurt}}$ & $\\rho_\\sigma$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nLAMBADA & 5153 & .30 & .10 & .09 & $-1.0^*$ \\\\\nARC-Easy & 2376 & .21 & .10 & .11 & $-0.2$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\footnotesize $^*p = .017$. $\\alpha$ stable across ratios (LAMBADA .29--.32; ARC-Easy .20--.23).}\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Decoupling}"
}

          "+Attention weight analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:attention_patterns}) provides partial insight: at layer 2, GDN and RetNet produce nearly identical attention distributions (cosine 0.995) yet differ in per-example effects; entropy differences between helped and hurt examples are negligible for GDN and HGRN2 ($|\\Delta H| \\leq 0.002$), with RetNet showing a small but slightly larger difference ($\\Delta H = +0.018$). {
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "72fa4ccf-e810-447e-8afd-ea779d5e7854",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "aee7e002-3eb5-424e-9cda-18af3d2bc2b1",
  "type": "user",
  "uuid": "c33a9575-54c1-47b2-949c-8ffadb1cc065",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_2/projects/entrainment_gated_ft/docs/paper/sections/method.tex",
    "oldString": "To move beyond aggregate metrics, we decompose performance changes at the individual-example level on LAMBADA (5{,}153 examples).",
    "newString": "To move beyond aggregate metrics, we decompose performance changes at the individual-example level on LAMBADA (5{,}153 examples) and ARC-Easy (2{,}376 examples).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\\label{sec:method}\n\nWe describe the model configurations, evaluation benchmarks, and analytical framework used to measure how attention affects cross-architecture agreement at both aggregate and per-example levels.\n\n\\subsection{Model Zoo}\\label{ssec:model_zoo}\n\nWe use the pretrained model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}, which provides controlled comparisons across architectures and attention ratios. The zoo contains three linear recurrent architectures: GatedDeltaNet \\citep{yang2024gateddeltanet}, RetNet \\citep{sun2023retnet}, and HGRN2 \\citep{qin2024hgrn2}. These three architectures represent distinct approaches to linear recurrence: GatedDeltaNet uses gated delta rules, RetNet uses multi-scale exponential decay, and HGRN2 uses gated linear recurrence with state expansion. Each architecture is trained at five attention ratios: 0\\% (pure recurrent), 4\\%, 7.7\\%, 14.3\\%, and 25\\%. We adopt the h$k$-1 notation from the original work, where one attention layer is interleaved per every $k{+}1$ total layers: h3-1 corresponds to 25\\% attention, h6-1 to 14.3\\%, h12-1 to 7.7\\%, and h24-1 to 4\\%. All configurations are available at two scales: 340M and 1.3B parameters. Within each scale, all models share identical training data, tokenizer, and hyperparameters; they differ only in architecture and attention configuration. This controlled design isolates the effect of attention from confounds such as training data or optimization differences. The zoo also includes a Transformer baseline (pure attention) at both scales.\n\n\\subsection{Task Taxonomy}\\label{ssec:task_taxonomy}\n\nWe evaluate on 10 benchmarks grouped into four categories defined by the primary cognitive demand of each task. This taxonomy was fixed prior to analysis (see Appendix~A for the confirmatory/exploratory split), not derived post hoc from observed results. The categories test whether the effect of attention on cross-architecture variance depends on task type.\n\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Retrieval}: LAMBADA \\citep{paperno2016lambada}, which requires predicting a final word from long-range context without reasoning or world knowledge.\n    \\item \\textbf{Comprehension}: BoolQ, a reading comprehension task with yes/no answers.\n    \\item \\textbf{Reasoning}: HellaSwag, ARC-Challenge \\citep{clark2018arc}, WinoGrande, and PIQA. COPA ($n = 100$) serves as a supplementary reasoning benchmark.\n    \\item \\textbf{Mixed}: ARC-Easy, MMLU, SciQ, and XStoryCloze, which combine retrieval, comprehension, and reasoning demands.\n\\end{itemize}\n\nAll evaluations use the lm-evaluation-harness framework \\citep{gao2024lmeval} with default prompts; accuracy serves as the primary metric for all benchmarks.\n\n\\subsection{Convergence Metrics}\\label{ssec:convergence_metrics}\n\nWe quantify cross-architecture agreement as a function of attention ratio. For each benchmark and attention ratio, we compute the cross-architecture standard deviation $\\sigma$ over the three architectures' accuracy scores. To measure the relationship between attention and convergence, we compute the Spearman rank correlation $\\rho$ between attention ratio rank and $\\sigma$ across the five attention configurations ($n = 5$). A negative $\\rho$ indicates that higher attention ratios are associated with lower cross-architecture variance.\n\nWe also report the convergence ratio $\\sigma_{\\text{pure}} / \\sigma_{\\text{h3-1}}$, defined as the standard deviation under the pure-recurrent configuration divided by that under the highest attention ratio (25\\%). Values greater than 1 indicate that attention reduces cross-architecture variance.\n\n\\subsection{Per-Example Decomposition}\\label{ssec:per_example_decomposition}\n\nTo move beyond aggregate metrics, we decompose performance changes at the individual-example level on LAMBADA (5{,}153 examples). For each architecture, we compare the pure-recurrent baseline against its h3-1 hybrid and classify each example into one of three categories:\n\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Helped}: correct in the hybrid but incorrect in the pure model.\n    \\item \\textbf{Hurt}: correct in the pure model but incorrect in the hybrid.\n    \\item \\textbf{Unchanged}: both models agree (both correct or both incorrect).\n\\end{itemize}\n\nThe net effect for each architecture is $|\\text{helped}| - |\\text{hurt}|$. A positive net effect means attention produces more correct predictions than it removes; a negative net effect means the opposite.\n\nTo measure cross-architecture overlap in per-example effects, we compute the pairwise Jaccard similarity $J = |A \\cap B| / |A \\cup B|$ between the helped sets of two architectures and, separately, between their hurt sets. $J = 1$ indicates identical sets; $J = 0$ indicates no overlap. Low $J$ values would indicate that different architectures are affected on different subsets of examples, which would constitute per-example divergence even when aggregate performance converges.\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Methods}\\label{ssec:statistical_methods}\n\nAll statistical tests are two-tailed unless otherwise noted.\n\n\\paragraph{Permutation test.} With only $n = 5$ attention ratios, we use an exact permutation test for $\\rho$. All $5! = 120$ permutations of the attention ratio ranks are enumerated, and the two-tailed $p$-value is the fraction of permutations yielding $|\\rho| \\geq |\\rho_{\\text{obs}}|$.\n\n\\paragraph{Bootstrap confidence intervals.} We construct 95\\% BCa (bias-corrected and accelerated) bootstrap confidence intervals for $\\rho$ using 10{,}000 resamples at the architecture level. When the BCa correction degenerates at a boundary value, we report percentile confidence intervals instead. With only $n = 5$ attention ratios, these intervals provide a complementary characterization of effect magnitude alongside the permutation test.\n\n\\paragraph{Multiple comparisons.} We apply Bonferroni correction across six confirmatory benchmarks (Appendix~A). The four remaining benchmarks are corrected separately across all 10 benchmarks and reported as exploratory.\n\n\\paragraph{Within- and cross-architecture tests.} For within-architecture comparisons of per-example properties (e.g., token count of helped vs.\\ hurt examples), we use the Mann-Whitney U test. For cross-architecture comparisons of per-example effect distributions, we use the Kruskal-Wallis test. Both are rank-based nonparametric tests, chosen because the per-example effect distributions are non-normal and contain ties.\n\n\\paragraph{Attention weight analysis.} To probe whether attention mechanisms differ between helped and hurt examples, we extract attention weight matrices from each attention layer via forward hooks and compute two quantities: (1) cosine similarity between the mean attention distributions of two architectures at each layer, which measures cross-architecture agreement in attention allocation; and (2) Shannon entropy of the attention distribution for each example, which measures how concentrated or diffuse the attention pattern is. Details of the extraction procedure appear in Appendix~D.\n",

  "old_string": "We present the first systematic per-example analysis of how attention affects cross-architecture agreement in hybrid recurrent models. Three findings emerge: convergence is task-specific (only LAMBADA among ten benchmarks); convergence depends on scale (complete at 1.3B, non-monotonic at 340M); and aggregate convergence and per-example divergence are decoupled---cross-architecture variance decreases gradually with attention, but per-example overlap saturates at the first attention level. This constitutes predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} from architectural variation: aggregate equivalence does not imply per-example interchangeability \\citep{damour2022underspecification}. The low-overlap per-example effect sets (Jaccard $\\leq 0.11$, approximately twice the independence baseline) suggest that ensembles of diverse hybrids may yield complementary coverage, and that deployment-critical applications should evaluate per-example agreement rather than relying on aggregate benchmarks alone. Future work could establish causal links through attention head ablation and test generality across other hybrid families.",
  "new_string": "We present the first systematic per-example analysis of how attention affects cross-architecture agreement in hybrid recurrent models. Three findings emerge: aggregate convergence is task-specific (only LAMBADA among ten benchmarks); convergence depends on scale (complete at 1.3B, non-monotonic at 340M); and per-example predictive multiplicity is general---approximately 21--30\\% of examples elicit cross-architecture disagreement on both LAMBADA and ARC-Easy ($\\alpha \\approx 0.21$--$0.30$), independent of aggregate convergence. This constitutes predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} from architectural variation \\citep{damour2022underspecification}. The low-overlap per-example effect sets (Jaccard $\\leq 0.13$) suggest that ensembles of diverse hybrids may yield complementary coverage, and that deployment-critical applications should evaluate per-example agreement rather than relying on aggregate benchmarks alone. Future work could establish causal links through attention head ablation and extend the analysis to additional hybrid families."
}

0	\section{Experiments}\label{sec:experiments}
1	
2	We analyze how attention affects cross-architecture performance variance using models from the hybrid recurrent model zoo of \citet{wang2025hybrid}. Our analysis spans three architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2), five attention ratios (0\% pure recurrent, 4\%, 7.7\%, 14.3\%, 25\%), and two scales (340M and 1.3B parameters). In the h$k$-1 notation used throughout, one attention layer is interleaved per every $k{+}1$ layers; h3-1 denotes 25\% attention. All models within each scale share identical training data and hyperparameters; they differ only in architecture and attention configuration. We evaluate on 10 benchmarks spanning retrieval, comprehension, reasoning, and mixed task categories, plus one supplementary benchmark (COPA, $n = 100$).
3	
```

225	All normalized effect sizes fall below 0.10, confirming that observed overlap reaches only 5--7\% of the theoretical maximum. The low Jaccard values cannot be attributed to set-size constraints.
226	
227	\section{Jaccard Permutation Test}\label{app:jaccard_permutation}
228	
229	To assess whether the observed pairwise Jaccard similarities exceed what would be expected under independence, we perform a permutation test. For each architecture pair at 1.3B h3-1, we hold the helped (or hurt) set sizes fixed and randomly assign example indices to each set (10{,}000 permutations, seed 42). Table~\ref{tab:jaccard_perm} reports the observed $J$, the null baseline $J_{\text{null}}$ (mean over permutations), and the $z$-score.
230	
231	\begin{table}[h]
232	\centering
233	\caption{Permutation test for pairwise Jaccard similarity (1.3B h3-1 on LAMBADA, 10{,}000 permutations). All observed values significantly exceed the independence baseline.}
234	\label{tab:jaccard_perm}
235	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
236	\begin{tabular}{llcccc}
237	\toprule
238	Category & Pair & $J_{\text{obs}}$ & $J_{\text{null}}$ & $z$ & $p$ \\
239	\midrule
240	Helped & GDN--HGRN2 & .100 & .051 & 7.11 & $<$.0001 \\
241	 & GDN--RetNet & .092 & .045 & 6.75 & $<$.0001 \\
242	 & HGRN2--RetNet & .108 & .049 & 8.61 & $<$.0001 \\
243	\midrule
244	Hurt & GDN--HGRN2 & .088 & .049 & 5.91 & $<$.0001 \\
245	 & GDN--RetNet & .103 & .056 & 6.85 & $<$.0001 \\
246	 & HGRN2--RetNet & .087 & .045 & 6.05 & $<$.0001 \\
247	\bottomrule
248	\end{tabular}%
249	}
250	\end{table}
251	
252	All six comparisons yield $z > 5.9$ ($p < .0001$). The null baseline averages $J_{\text{null}} \approx 0.049$; observed values are approximately twice this level. The overlap between architectures' per-example effect sets is therefore structured rather than a product of random set overlap given the set sizes.
253	
254	\section{Transformer Entrainment Comparison}\label{app:transformer_entrainment}
255	
256	To test whether attention layers make hybrid models' per-example behavior converge toward a pure Transformer, we compare the correct-example sets of each architecture's pure and h3-1 variants against a 1.3B Transformer baseline (accuracy 48.1\%) on LAMBADA. Table~\ref{tab:transformer_jaccard} reports the Jaccard similarity between each model's correct set and the Transformer's correct set.

The overlap between architectures' per-example effect sets is therefore structured rather than a product of random set overlap given the set sizes.\n\n\\section{Transformer Entrainment Comparison}\\label{app:transformer_entrainment}\n\nTo test whether attention layers make hybrid models' per-example behavior converge toward a pure Transformer, we compare the correct-example sets of each architecture's pure and h3-1 variants against a 1.3B Transformer baseline (accuracy 48.1\\%) on LAMBADA. 273	
274	All three architectures show positive $\Delta$: the h3-1 correct set is more similar to the Transformer correct set than the pure correct set is. The effect is strongest for HGRN2 ($+$0.029) and weakest for GDN ($+$0.005). This ordering is consistent with HGRN2 being the only architecture with a positive net per-example effect from attention (Table~\ref{tab:per_example}). The Transformer itself achieves the lowest accuracy among all models, so the convergence is not explained by hybrid models degrading toward Transformer-level performance; rather, adding attention shifts which specific examples the hybrid gets correct toward the Transformer's pattern.
275	
276	\section{Within-Architecture Per-Example Stability}\label{app:within_arch_stability}
277	
278	Per-example effect sets exhibit a three-tier Jaccard hierarchy on LAMBADA (1.3B scale). Under independence (permutation baseline), pairwise Jaccard is $J \approx 0.05$. Cross-architecture comparisons (different architectures at the same attention ratio) yield $J \approx 0.10$, approximately $2\times$ baseline. Within-architecture comparisons (same architecture across different attention ratios) yield $J \approx 0.33$, approximately $7\times$ baseline and $3\times$ the cross-architecture level. Architecture identity therefore has approximately three times the influence of attention dose on per-example effects.
279	
280	Table~\ref{tab:within_arch_summary} reports mean within-architecture Jaccard for each architecture. HGRN2 shows the highest helped overlap and lowest hurt overlap, consistent with its monotonically increasing helped count across ratios (479 $\to$ 548).

326	Adjacent ratio pairs (e.g., h24-1 vs.\ h12-1) do not exhibit systematically higher Jaccard than non-adjacent pairs (e.g., h24-1 vs.\ h3-1). Across all architectures, the mean Jaccard for adjacent pairs is 0.353 (helped) and 0.311 (hurt), compared with 0.343 (helped) and 0.310 (hurt) for non-adjacent pairs. The negligible difference indicates that the mapping from attention dose to per-example effects is non-smooth: doubling the attention ratio does not produce a proportionally more similar set of affected examples.
327	
328	\section{Target-Token Confidence Margin}
329	\label{app:confidence_margin}
330	
331	We analyze whether pure-model confidence on the target token predicts whether attention will help or hurt on a given example.
332	We define confidence as $|\log p_{\text{pure}}(y^*)|$, where $y^*$ is the target token: lower values indicate higher probability (near-correct).
333	To avoid the definitional confound of comparing pure-correct (hurt) against pure-wrong (helped) samples, we perform within-category controls.
334	
335	Table~\ref{tab:cm_c1} compares hurt versus stayed-right samples (both pure-correct): hurt samples have significantly lower target-token probability (higher $|\log p|$), confirming that weakly-correct predictions are vulnerable to attention disruption.
336	Table~\ref{tab:cm_c2} compares helped versus stayed-wrong samples (both pure-wrong): helped samples have higher target-token probability (lower $|\log p|$), confirming that near-miss errors are rescuable.
337	This pattern is consistent across all three architectures ($|d| = 0.73$--$0.92$, all $p < 10^{-54}$) but does not alter the cross-architecture Jaccard findings---within-architecture predictability coexists with cross-architecture divergence.

276	\section{Jaccard Permutation Test}\label{app:jaccard_permutation}
277	
278	To assess whether the observed pairwise Jaccard similarities exceed what would be expected under independence, we perform a permutation test. For each architecture pair at 1.3B h3-1, we hold the helped (or hurt) set sizes fixed and randomly assign example indices to each set (10{,}000 permutations, seed 42). Table~\ref{tab:jaccard_perm} reports the observed $J$, the null baseline $J_{\text{null}}$ (mean over permutations), and the $z$-score.
```

This session is being continued from a previous conversation that ran out of context. The summary below covers the earlier portion of the conversation.

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   The Director issued three sequential patch instructions for an EMNLP 2026 ARR submission (paper: "Task-Specific Convergence and Per-Example Predictive Multiplicity in Hybrid Recurrent Architectures"):
   
   **Task A (completed)**: Continue page compression from prior session to fit main text §1-§5 within 8 pages.
   
   **Task B (completed)**: Round 2 wording fixes + citation additions:
   - A3: Add Benjamini-Hochberg correction sentence
   - A4: Add Fehlauer et al. (EMNLP 2025) and Sellam et al. (ICLR 2022) to Related Work
   - A6: Fix 4 wording issues (W5 "nearly disjoint", W7 attention overreach, W2 scale consistency, Abstract "is fixed")
   
   **Task C (in progress)**: Major patch — ARC-Easy Per-Example + Formal Multiplicity Metrics. Core narrative upgrade: "per-example multiplicity is a universal property of hybrid architectures, aggregate convergence is task-specific." This involves rewriting Abstract, Introduction Finding 3, contributions, adding §4.3 cross-task generalization paragraph with Table 2, updating §4.4 Decoupling, compressing §4.5 Robustness, updating Conclusion, Limitations, Method, and adding appendix section.

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - **Predictive Multiplicity** (Marx et al. 2020): Competing models produce conflicting predictions on individual inputs despite similar aggregate scores
   - **Ambiguity α**: Fraction of examples on which at least one architecture disagrees — LAMBADA α≈0.30, ARC-Easy α≈0.21, stable across attention ratios
   - **Jaccard similarity**: Pairwise overlap of helped/hurt sets — LAMBADA ≤0.11, ARC-Easy 0.067-0.134
   - **Decoupling**: Aggregate σ decreases gradually while per-example Jaccard saturates at 4% attention
   - **ARC-Easy key data**: N=2376, ρ=-0.20, p=.783 (no convergence), all 24 Jaccard pair×ratio combinations p<.01
   - **EMNLP 2026**: 8-page main text limit (§1-§5), unlimited for references/limitations/ethics/appendix
   - **Bonferroni-adjusted p=.100**, BH-adjusted p=0.102 — neither survives strict correction
   - **BCa 95% CI [-1.0, -0.7]** excludes zero — complementary characterization

3. Files and Code Sections:

   - **`/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_2/projects/entrainment_gated_ft/docs/paper/main.tex`**
     - Contains title, abstract, limitations, ethics, and includes for all sections
     - Abstract fully rewritten to include ARC-Easy: "Aggregate convergence is task-specific... Per-example predictive multiplicity, however, is general..."
     - Limitations §Per-example scope: changed from "limited to LAMBADA" to "covers two benchmarks (LAMBADA, ARC-Easy)"
     - "per-example specificity is fixed" → "is established" (Round 2 fix)

   - **`/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_2/projects/entrainment_gated_ft/docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex`**
     - P3: Updated scope to "LAMBADA ($N = 5{,}153$) and ARC-Easy ($N = 2{,}376$)"
     - Finding 3 rewritten: "per-example predictive multiplicity is a general property of hybrid architectures, independent of aggregate convergence" with LAMBADA decoupling + ARC-Easy no-convergence contrast + α metric
     - Contribution bullet 3: "per-example predictive multiplicity is a general property (ambiguity α ≈ 0.21--0.30 across retrieval and reasoning tasks)"
     - "almost entirely different examples" → "different examples with low overlap" (Round 2 W5 fix)

   - **`/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_2/projects/entrainment_gated_ft/docs/paper/sections/experiments.tex`**
     - §4.1: Added BH sentence; updated last sentence to reference ARC-Easy extending per-example divergence
     - §4.3 opening: Compressed from 2 sentences to shorter form
     - §4.3: Added "Cross-task generalization" paragraph with ARC-Easy analysis + Table 2 (multiplicity):
       ```latex
       \begin{table}[t]
       \centering
       \small
       \caption{Predictive multiplicity at 1.3B (h3-1). $\alpha$: ambiguity \citep{marx2020predictive}. $\bar{J}$: mean pairwise Jaccard of helped~(h) and hurt~(u) sets.}

{
  "parentUuid": "4d362ab9-45d0-45bb-984e-09f37acbee9c",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_2/projects/entrainment_gated_ft/docs/paper/sections/experiments.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_2/projects/entrainment_gated_ft/docs/paper/sections/experiments.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Experiments}\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nWe analyze how attention affects cross-architecture performance variance using models from the hybrid recurrent model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}. Our analysis spans three architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2), five attention ratios (0\\% pure recurrent, 4\\%, 7.7\\%, 14.3\\%, 25\\%), and two scales (340M and 1.3B parameters). In the h$k$-1 notation used throughout, one attention layer is interleaved per every $k{+}1$ layers; h3-1 denotes 25\\% attention. All models within each scale share identical training data and hyperparameters; they differ only in architecture and attention configuration. We evaluate on 10 benchmarks spanning retrieval, comprehension, reasoning, and mixed task categories, plus one supplementary benchmark (COPA, $n = 100$).\n\n\\subsection{Task-Specific Convergence}\\label{ssec:task_convergence}\n\nWe first ask whether adding attention layers reduces performance differences between architectures, and whether any such reduction is uniform across tasks. For each benchmark, we compute the cross-architecture standard deviation ($\\sigma$) at each attention ratio and measure the Spearman rank correlation ($\\rho$) between attention ratio and $\\sigma$ across the five configurations.\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:convergence} reports results for all benchmarks at 1.3B scale. LAMBADA is the only benchmark exhibiting monotonic convergence: $\\rho = -1.0$ (exact permutation test, $p = .017$, two-tailed, $n = 5$, 120 permutations). Cross-architecture $\\sigma$ drops from 0.024 to 0.005, a 79\\% reduction. The 95\\% BCa percentile bootstrap confidence interval for $\\rho$ is $[-1.0, -0.7]$ (10{,}000 resamples), which excludes zero. After Bonferroni correction for six confirmatory benchmarks, the adjusted $p = .100$ does not survive strict Bonferroni correction at $\\alpha = 0.05$. Benjamini--Hochberg false discovery rate control yields a similar adjusted $p$ (0.102), as only one of six benchmarks approaches nominal significance. The confidence interval provides a complementary characterization of the convergence effect, indicating a reliably negative association even though the corrected $p$-value does not reach conventional significance.\n\nLAMBADA is a pure context-retrieval task \\citep{paperno2016lambada}; attention provides direct token-level access to the full context, consistent with convergence reflecting compensation for differences in how recurrent architectures store contextual information. No other benchmark shows significant convergence (all $|\\rho| \\leq 0.7$, $p \\geq .233$). ARC-Easy and MMLU exhibit moderate negative correlations ($\\rho = -0.7$) but non-monotonic trajectories; COPA shows a similar pattern ($\\rho = -0.67$) but is limited by $n = 100$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:task_specificity}). The decoupling analysis (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}) requires a converging benchmark and is demonstrated on LAMBADA; per-example divergence itself extends to ARC-Easy (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_task_specificity.pdf}\n\\caption{Spearman $\\rho$ between attention ratio and cross-architecture $\\sigma$ for each benchmark at 1.3B scale. LAMBADA ($\\rho = -1.0$; BCa 95\\% CI $[-1.0, -0.7]$) is the only benchmark with a negative correlation whose confidence interval excludes zero. Benchmarks are grouped by task category.}\n\\label{fig:task_specificity}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cross-architecture convergence across benchmarks (1.3B scale). $\\rho$: Spearman correlation between attention ratio and cross-architecture $\\sigma$ across five configurations. $p$: exact permutation test (two-tailed, 120 permutations). $\\sigma$ values reported $\\times 10^{2}$ (accuracy percentage points). Ratio: $\\sigma_{\\text{pure}} / \\sigma_{\\text{h3-1}}$. \\textbf{Bold}: only benchmark with $\\rho = -1.0$.}\n\\label{tab:convergence}\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrrr}\n\\toprule\nBenchmark & Category & $\\rho$ & $p$ & $\\sigma_{\\text{pure}}$ & $\\sigma_{\\text{h3-1}}$ & Ratio \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\textbf{LAMBADA} & \\textbf{Retrieval} & $\\mathbf{-1.0}$ & \\textbf{.017} & \\textbf{2.41} & \\textbf{0.49} & \\textbf{4.87} \\\\\nBoolQ & Comprehension & $0.2$ & .783 & 1.63 & 2.36 & 0.69 \\\\\nARC-C & Reasoning & $0.6$ & .350 & 0.96 & 1.32 & 0.72 \\\\\nHellaSwag & Reasoning & $0.3$ & .683 & 1.17 & 1.18 & 0.99 \\\\\nPIQA & Reasoning & $-0.5$ & .450 & 0.54 & 0.34 & 1.59 \\\\\nWinoGrande & Reasoning & $0.1$ & .950 & 0.67 & 0.78 & 0.86 \\\\\nARC-E & Mixed & $-0.7$ & .233 & 1.00 & 0.13 & 7.70 \\\\\nMMLU & Mixed & $-0.7$ & .233 & 0.92 & 0.41 & 2.27 \\\\\nSciQ & Mixed & $0.1$ & .950 & 0.87 & 0.74 & 1.19 \\\\\nXStoryCloze & Mixed & $-0.6$ & .350 & 0.82 & 0.55 & 1.49 \\\\\n\\midrule\nCOPA$^{\\dagger}$ & Reasoning & $-0.67$ & .267 & 2.63 & 0.47 & 5.57 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\footnotesize $^{\\dagger}$Supplementary ($n = 100$); excluded from multiple comparison correction.}\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\subsection{Scale-Dependent Convergence}\\label{ssec:scale_convergence}\n\nWe compare the LAMBADA convergence pattern at two scales. At 1.3B parameters, convergence is monotonic: $\\sigma$ decreases at every attention level from 0\\% through 25\\% ($\\rho = -1.0$). At 340M, convergence is partial. $\\sigma$ reaches a maximum reduction of 56\\% at intermediate attention ratios, but rebounds at h3-1, yielding a net endpoint reduction of 36\\%: the trajectory is non-monotonic, and the highest-attention model does not achieve the lowest cross-architecture variance. One possible interpretation is that at smaller scale, the capacity overhead of attention layers creates interference that offsets the retrieval benefit.\n\nThis contrast is a preliminary observation from two data points (not a scaling law) that scale may facilitate complete equalization. Despite the aggregate divergence, mean pairwise Jaccard similarity is similar at both scales (340M: $J = 0.094$ helped, $0.090$ hurt; 1.3B: $0.100$, $0.093$), extending the decoupling observation (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}) to the scale dimension.\n\n\\subsection{Per-Example Divergence}\\label{ssec:per_example}\n\nDo architectures converge by improving on the same examples? We compare each architecture's pure-recurrent baseline against its h3-1 hybrid on 5{,}153 LAMBADA examples, classifying each as helped (correct only in hybrid), hurt (correct only in pure), or unchanged.\n\nEach architecture displays a distinct profile of per-example changes (Table~\\ref{tab:per_example}). GDN loses a net 153 examples (462 helped, 615 hurt), RetNet loses 69 (426/495), and HGRN2 gains 153 (548/395). HGRN2 is the only architecture for which attention produces a net per-example benefit. Pairwise Jaccard similarity is low: mean $\\bar{J} \\leq 0.104$ for helped, $\\leq 0.096$ for hurt, indicating ${\\sim}90$\\% non-overlap. Overlap significantly exceeds the independence baseline ($J_{\\text{null}} \\approx 0.05$, all $z > 5.9$, $p < .0001$; Appendix~\\ref{app:jaccard_permutation}), but normalizing by the theoretical upper bound $J_{\\max} = \\min(|A|,|B|)/\\max(|A|,|B|)$, observed overlap reaches only 5--7\\% of the achievable range above chance (normalized ES 0.065 helped, 0.062 hurt; per-pair details in Appendix~\\ref{app:normalized_es}), confirming that low Jaccard cannot be attributed to set-size constraints.\n\nNo surface-level feature (token count, word frequency, syntactic complexity) reliably separates helped from hurt examples. However, pure-model confidence on the target token is strongly predictive within each architecture: hurt examples have lower target-token probability among pure-correct examples ($d = 0.83$--$0.92$), and helped examples have higher probability among pure-wrong examples ($d = 0.73$--$0.77$); see Appendix~\\ref{app:confidence_margin}. A Kruskal-Wallis test confirms cross-architecture structure in both hurt ($p = .0005$) and helped ($p = .020$) distributions. This structured per-example disagreement despite similar aggregate performance constitutes predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} arising from architectural variation rather than random initialization \\citep{damour2022underspecification}.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-task generalization.} To test whether per-example divergence depends on aggregate convergence, we repeat the analysis on ARC-Easy ($N = 2{,}376$), a mixed benchmark with no aggregate convergence ($\\rho = -0.20$, $p = .783$; $\\sigma$ flat at ${\\sim}0.10$). Pairwise Jaccard ranges from 0.067 to 0.134 (all 24 pair $\\times$ ratio combinations $p < .01$, permutation test). Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity} reports the ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive}---the fraction of examples with cross-architecture disagreement. Ambiguity is substantial on both benchmarks ($\\alpha \\approx 0.21$--$0.30$) and stable across attention ratios, confirming that per-example multiplicity is not contingent on aggregate convergence.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Predictive multiplicity at 1.3B (h3-1). $\\alpha$: ambiguity \\citep{marx2020predictive}. $\\bar{J}$: mean pairwise Jaccard of helped~(h) and hurt~(u) sets.}\n\\label{tab:multiplicity}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lrcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n & $N$ & $\\alpha$ & $\\bar{J}_{h}$ & $\\bar{J}_{u}$ & $\\rho_\\sigma$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nLAMBADA & 5153 & .30 & .10 & .09 & $-1.0^*$ \\\\\nARC-Easy & 2376 & .21 & .10 & .11 & $-0.2$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\par\\vspace{1pt}\n{\\footnotesize $^*\\!p = .017$. $\\alpha$ stable across ratios (.29--.32 / .20--.23).}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Decoupling}\\label{ssec:decoupling}\n\nThe central finding of this paper is that aggregate convergence and per-example divergence operate on different timescales as attention ratio increases (Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling}).\n\nCross-architecture $\\sigma$ decreases gradually across the five attention ratios: $0.024 \\to 0.016 \\to 0.014 \\to 0.006 \\to 0.005$. Each additional attention layer provides incremental reduction. In contrast, pairwise Jaccard similarity between architectures' per-example effects saturates at the first attention level (4\\% attention): approximately 0.095 for helped examples, with no further increase at higher ratios. Net asymmetry between architectures' per-example profiles increases from 0.013 at 4\\% attention to 0.040 at 25\\%.\n\nThis separation of timescales constitutes a decoupling: attention reduces the aggregate performance gap but does not homogenize per-example behavior. Bootstrap confidence intervals (10{,}000 resamples) confirm this statistically: the 95\\% CI for $\\sigma$ does not overlap between pure $[0.019, 0.030]$ and h3-1 $[0.002, 0.011]$, while the CIs for mean Jaccard overlap completely across all non-zero attention ratios (helped: $[0.08, 0.11]$; hurt: $[0.07, 0.11]$). Within-architecture Jaccard averages ${\\sim}0.33$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:within_arch_stability}), roughly three times the cross-architecture value, confirming that architecture identity has greater influence than attention dose on which examples are affected.\n\nAttention weight analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:attention_patterns}) provides partial insight: at layer 2, GDN and RetNet produce nearly identical attention distributions (cosine 0.995) yet differ in per-example effects; entropy differences between helped and hurt examples are negligible for GDN and HGRN2 ($|\\Delta H| \\leq 0.002$), with RetNet showing a small but slightly larger difference ($\\Delta H = +0.018$). Per-example effects are not explained by attention weight patterns, consistent with originating in the recurrent layers. On ARC-Easy, where aggregate performance shows no convergence ($\\rho = -0.20$, n.s.), per-example ambiguity remains comparable ($\\alpha \\approx 0.21$ vs.\\ 0.30 on LAMBADA; Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}), confirming that per-example architectural disagreement does not depend on aggregate equalization.\n\n\\subsection{Robustness}\\label{ssec:robustness}\n\nRemoving GDN weakens the LAMBADA convergence from $\\rho = -1.0$ to $\\rho = -0.7$ ($p = .233$), partly confounded with reduced statistical resolution at $n = 2$ architectures. The RetNet--HGRN2 pair preserves the decoupling: all hybrid $\\sigma \\leq 0.008$ ($>$54\\% below pure) while Jaccard saturates at 0.086--0.108, confirming the result is not an artifact of the GDN head-count confound (Appendix~\\ref{app:gdn_confound}). Full sensitivity analyses appear in Appendix~\\ref{app:sensitivity}.\n",
        "numLines": 91,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 91
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/sections/experiments.tex"
  },

        "content": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\nWe present the first systematic per-example analysis of how attention affects cross-architecture agreement in hybrid recurrent models. Three findings emerge: aggregate convergence is task-specific (only LAMBADA among ten benchmarks); convergence depends on scale (complete at 1.3B, non-monotonic at 340M); and per-example predictive multiplicity is general---approximately 21--30\\% of examples elicit cross-architecture disagreement on both LAMBADA and ARC-Easy ($\\alpha \\approx 0.21$--$0.30$), independent of aggregate convergence. This constitutes predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} from architectural variation \\citep{damour2022underspecification}. The low-overlap per-example effect sets (Jaccard $\\leq 0.13$) suggest that ensembles of diverse hybrids may yield complementary coverage, and that deployment-critical applications should evaluate per-example agreement rather than relying on aggregate benchmarks alone. Future work could establish causal links through attention head ablation and extend the analysis to additional hybrid families.\n",
        "numLines": 4,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 4
      }
    },

  "parentUuid": "1594a998-1801-48a6-b392-09e84aabfc5d",

  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_2/projects/entrainment_gated_ft/docs/paper/sections/method.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_2/projects/entrainment_gated_ft/docs/paper/sections/method.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Method}\\label{sec:method}\n\nWe describe the model configurations, evaluation benchmarks, and analytical framework used to measure how attention affects cross-architecture agreement at both aggregate and per-example levels.\n\n\\subsection{Model Zoo}\\label{ssec:model_zoo}\n\nWe use the pretrained model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}, which provides controlled comparisons across architectures and attention ratios. The zoo contains three linear recurrent architectures: GatedDeltaNet \\citep{yang2024gateddeltanet}, RetNet \\citep{sun2023retnet}, and HGRN2 \\citep{qin2024hgrn2}. These three architectures represent distinct approaches to linear recurrence: GatedDeltaNet uses gated delta rules, RetNet uses multi-scale exponential decay, and HGRN2 uses gated linear recurrence with state expansion. Each architecture is trained at five attention ratios: 0\\% (pure recurrent), 4\\%, 7.7\\%, 14.3\\%, and 25\\%. We adopt the h$k$-1 notation from the original work, where one attention layer is interleaved per every $k{+}1$ total layers: h3-1 corresponds to 25\\% attention, h6-1 to 14.3\\%, h12-1 to 7.7\\%, and h24-1 to 4\\%. All configurations are available at two scales: 340M and 1.3B parameters. Within each scale, all models share identical training data, tokenizer, and hyperparameters; they differ only in architecture and attention configuration. This controlled design isolates the effect of attention from confounds such as training data or optimization differences. The zoo also includes a Transformer baseline (pure attention) at both scales.\n\n\\subsection{Task Taxonomy}\\label{ssec:task_taxonomy}\n\nWe evaluate on 10 benchmarks grouped into four categories defined by the primary cognitive demand of each task. This taxonomy was fixed prior to analysis (see Appendix~A for the confirmatory/exploratory split), not derived post hoc from observed results. The categories test whether the effect of attention on cross-architecture variance depends on task type.\n\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Retrieval}: LAMBADA \\citep{paperno2016lambada}, which requires predicting a final word from long-range context without reasoning or world knowledge.\n    \\item \\textbf{Comprehension}: BoolQ, a reading comprehension task with yes/no answers.\n    \\item \\textbf{Reasoning}: HellaSwag, ARC-Challenge \\citep{clark2018arc}, WinoGrande, and PIQA. COPA ($n = 100$) serves as a supplementary reasoning benchmark.\n    \\item \\textbf{Mixed}: ARC-Easy, MMLU, SciQ, and XStoryCloze, which combine retrieval, comprehension, and reasoning demands.\n\\end{itemize}\n\nAll evaluations use the lm-evaluation-harness framework \\citep{gao2024lmeval} with default prompts; accuracy serves as the primary metric for all benchmarks.\n\n\\subsection{Convergence Metrics}\\label{ssec:convergence_metrics}\n\nWe quantify cross-architecture agreement as a function of attention ratio. For each benchmark and attention ratio, we compute the cross-architecture standard deviation $\\sigma$ over the three architectures' accuracy scores. To measure the relationship between attention and convergence, we compute the Spearman rank correlation $\\rho$ between attention ratio rank and $\\sigma$ across the five attention configurations ($n = 5$). A negative $\\rho$ indicates that higher attention ratios are associated with lower cross-architecture variance.\n\nWe also report the convergence ratio $\\sigma_{\\text{pure}} / \\sigma_{\\text{h3-1}}$, defined as the standard deviation under the pure-recurrent configuration divided by that under the highest attention ratio (25\\%). Values greater than 1 indicate that attention reduces cross-architecture variance.\n\n\\subsection{Per-Example Decomposition}\\label{ssec:per_example_decomposition}\n\nTo move beyond aggregate metrics, we decompose performance changes at the individual-example level on LAMBADA (5{,}153 examples) and ARC-Easy (2{,}376 examples). For each architecture, we compare the pure-recurrent baseline against its h3-1 hybrid and classify each example into one of three categories:\n\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Helped}: correct in the hybrid but incorrect in the pure model.\n    \\item \\textbf{Hurt}: correct in the pure model but incorrect in the hybrid.\n    \\item \\textbf{Unchanged}: both models agree (both correct or both incorrect).\n\\end{itemize}\n\nThe net effect for each architecture is $|\\text{helped}| - |\\text{hurt}|$. A positive net effect means attention produces more correct predictions than it removes; a negative net effect means the opposite.\n\nTo measure cross-architecture overlap in per-example effects, we compute the pairwise Jaccard similarity $J = |A \\cap B| / |A \\cup B|$ between the helped sets of two architectures and, separately, between their hurt sets. $J = 1$ indicates identical sets; $J = 0$ indicates no overlap. Low $J$ values would indicate that different architectures are affected on different subsets of examples, which would constitute per-example divergence even when aggregate performance converges.\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Methods}\\label{ssec:statistical_methods}\n\nAll statistical tests are two-tailed unless otherwise noted.\n\n\\paragraph{Permutation test.} With only $n = 5$ attention ratios, we use an exact permutation test for $\\rho$. All $5! = 120$ permutations of the attention ratio ranks are enumerated, and the two-tailed $p$-value is the fraction of permutations yielding $|\\rho| \\geq |\\rho_{\\text{obs}}|$.\n\n\\paragraph{Bootstrap confidence intervals.} We construct 95\\% BCa (bias-corrected and accelerated) bootstrap confidence intervals for $\\rho$ using 10{,}000 resamples at the architecture level. When the BCa correction degenerates at a boundary value, we report percentile confidence intervals instead. With only $n = 5$ attention ratios, these intervals provide a complementary characterization of effect magnitude alongside the permutation test.\n\n\\paragraph{Multiple comparisons.} We apply Bonferroni correction across six confirmatory benchmarks (Appendix~A). The four remaining benchmarks are corrected separately across all 10 benchmarks and reported as exploratory.\n\n\\paragraph{Within- and cross-architecture tests.} For within-architecture comparisons of per-example properties (e.g., token count of helped vs.\\ hurt examples), we use the Mann-Whitney U test. For cross-architecture comparisons of per-example effect distributions, we use the Kruskal-Wallis test. Both are rank-based nonparametric tests, chosen because the per-example effect distributions are non-normal and contain ties.\n\n\\paragraph{Attention weight analysis.} To probe whether attention mechanisms differ between helped and hurt examples, we extract attention weight matrices from each attention layer via forward hooks and compute two quantities: (1) cosine similarity between the mean attention distributions of two architectures at each layer, which measures cross-architecture agreement in attention allocation; and (2) Shannon entropy of the attention distribution for each example, which measures how concentrated or diffuse the attention pattern is. Details of the extraction procedure appear in Appendix~D.\n",
        "numLines": 55,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 55
      }
    },

Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching.
